========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
A month? No, no, it's been way longer than a month. It's been about a year and a month, actually. I know because it started right around the time I was trying to clean up the little patio area they give us. 

I had to go buy a new rake, and I walked down to that big box store on the highway. I usually hate those places. The aisles are too wide and you can never find anybody to help you. Back when I worked hardware, we actually talked to the customers, you know? We knew what we were talking about. Now it's just teenagers looking at their phones and self-checkout machines barking at you to bag your items. And the tools... don't get me started. I bought this plastic rake because it was all they had, and it was a complete piece of junk. Snapped on me in two weeks. You need solid wood and steel for a real tool. 

But anyway, yeah, it was right around that time, a year and a month ago, that I first caught on to them watching me and slipping those notes around.

Everybody keeps telling me it's just regular notices on the doors, like for maintenance or whatever, but I know what it really is. Speaking of maintenance, the guy who fixes the pipes, he came by last Tuesday. He had this wrench that looked like it belonged in a museum, I swear. I collect old tools, you know. Voices? No, I've never heard any voices. Well, except for the neighbors talking through the walls, but that's just because the walls are paper thin in that place. You can hear everything. I remember when I lived in this old brick building downtown back in the 90s, you couldn't hear a peep from anyone. Solid construction, you know? They don't build them like that anymore. Now it's all drywall and cheap insulation. I tried telling the landlord about it, but he just brushed me off. Said it was normal apartment living. Normal? Hah. I know what normal is, and having your neighbors sound like they're in your living room isn't it. I used to do some framing work myself, before the hardware store. We always made sure to use the right materials. But no, no voices that aren't there, just the real ones that are way too loud.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Suspicious? Well, I mean, I guess I've always been a little careful, you know? You have to be. But not like this whole thing with the apartment building. That's different. But, you know, being on guard... it reminds me of this security guard we had at the hardware store back in '88. Guy's name was Frank. Frank used to walk the aisles like he was patrolling a military base. He had this big, heavy flashlight, the kind that takes like six D batteries. You don't see those much anymore, everything's LED now and rechargeable. Which is fine, I guess, but there was something about the weight of those old flashlights. You felt like you actually had something in your hand. Frank would shine that thing into every dark corner of the stockroom. He was always convinced kids were sneaking in to steal spray paint. Anyway, I wouldn't say I've been suspicious my whole life, no. Just... you know, paying attention to what's going on around me. Like Frank.

Frank used to walk the aisles like he was patrolling a military base. He had this big, heavy flashlight, the kind that takes like six D batteries. You felt like you actually had something in your hand. Frank would shine that thing into every dark corner of the stockroom. He was always convinced kids were sneaking in to steal spray paint. Like Frank.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you generally preferred being alone over being with other people?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Being alone? Yeah, mostly. I mean, I don't really go out of my way to be around crowds or anything. It's just easier that way, you know? Less hassle. I remember back when I was a kid, my old man would drag me to these massive family reunions out by the lake. Hundreds of people, cousins I didn't even know existed. Everyone was always yelling over each other, trying to get to the potato salad. My aunt Martha made the best potato salad, though. She put a little bit of mustard in it, gave it this real nice tang. You can't buy potato salad like that at the deli counter anymore. They always put too much mayonnaise in it now, makes it all soggy and heavy. I tried making it myself a few years back, bought the potatoes, the celery, the whole nine yards. But I ended up boiling the potatoes too long and it just turned into this big bowl of mush. So I just threw it out. But yeah, I guess I do like being by myself most of the time. Just me and my own space.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Unusual beliefs? No, I don't think so. I mean, I see things the way they are. People might think it's odd that I don't trust the water company, but that's just common sense. Have you seen what they put in the water these days? Fluoride, they say. Back in the day, you just drank from the tap and didn't think twice about it. I remember drinking straight from the garden hose in the summer when I was a kid. The water always tasted a little bit like rubber and dirt, but it was cold. We had this big green hose, must have been fifty feet long. My dad bought it at Sears. Sears used to be the place to go for everything. Tools, appliances, clothes. You could spend a whole Saturday just walking around Sears. I bought a socket set there in '82, Craftsman. Still have it. You can't beat that lifetime warranty. I tried to take a broken ratchet back to one of those new hardware stores last year, and the kid behind the counter looked at me like I had two heads. Didn't even know what a warranty was. Just told me I had to buy a new one. It's all about making a quick buck now. Nobody stands behind their products anymore. So, no, no unusual beliefs. Just... knowing how things used to be better, I guess.

I remember drinking straight from the garden hose in the summer when I was a kid. The water always tasted a little bit like rubber and dirt, but it was cold. We had this big green hose, must have been fifty feet long. My dad bought it at Sears. Sears used to be the place to go for everything. Tools, appliances, clothes. I tried to take a broken ratchet back to one of those new hardware stores last year, and the kid behind the counter looked at me like I had two heads. Didn't even know what a warranty was. Just told me I had to buy a new one. It's all about making a quick buck now. Nobody stands behind their products anymore. So, no, no unusual beliefs. Just... knowing how things used to be better, I guess.\n\n[Last Question]\nBefore this last year, did you tend to distrust or suspect other people’s motives for most of your adult life?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. Distrust people? Most of my life? No, I wouldn't say that. I mean, I've always just minded my own business, you know? Kept my head down. It's like when I used to work at the bottling plant back in the early nineties. You just did your shift and went home. The line boss there, this guy named Sal, he was always riding everybody. He had this little clipboard he carried around everywhere. Sal thought he was some big shot because he got to wear a white hard hat instead of a yellow one. I never understood why the color of a piece of plastic made someone think they were better than you. Hard hats are just to protect your skull, right? But Sal, he'd strut around... he drove this beat-up old Chevy Nova, faded blue, missing a hubcap on the back right tire. I always noticed that hubcap because I used to park my Ford Fairmont two spots down from him. That Fairmont was a good car. Had the bench seats in the front, the kind you don't see anymore. Everything's bucket seats now with that big center console taking up all the room. You can't even slide over to get out the passenger side if someone parks too close to you. It's ridiculous. But no, I didn't really distrust people before all this started. Just... you know, I just kept to myself mostly.

apartment checking the window over and over.\n- key: S031\n  name: Diminished_Thinking\n  description: Diminished ability to think or concentrate, or indecisiveness.\n  manifestation: Bills and appointment cards pile up because I read the same line again and again and still can't decide what needs doing first.\n- key: S032\n  name: Death_Suicide\n  description: Recurrent thoughts of death, recurrent suicidal ideation, or a suicide attempt.\n  manifestation: I don't have a plan to hurt myself, but I do catch myself thinking it would be all right if I just didn't wake up and had nothing more to mess up.\n- key: additional_requirement_1\n  name: Additional Requirement 1\n  description: psychotic_symptoms and depressive_episode occur concurrently\n  manifestation: When I am down in that hollow place and barely eating or answering the phone, I am also most sure the apartment people are tracking me and talking to the office about me.\n- key: additional_requirement_2\n  name: Additional Requirement 2\n  description: depressive_episode is present for the majority of the total duration of the active and residual portions of the illness (Criterion C)\n  manifestation: For most of the time since I moved, even on days when I could sound a little clearer, I still felt low, useless, and uninterested in the things I used to do.\n- key: additional_requirement_3\n  name: Additional Requirement 3\n  description: delusions_and_hallucinations occur for 2 or more weeks in the absence of a major mood episode (Criterion B)\n  manifestation: There was a spell of several weeks when I was eating a bit better and not waking up so hopeless, but I still believed the neighbors were watching my door and reporting on me.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  description: required\n  manifestation: I have fallen behind on bills, missed appointments, and stopped showing up for church and volunteer commitments because I cannot keep my days organized or trust ordinary interactions.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 1 year and 5 months\n  manifestation: This has been going on for about a year and five months, since I left the house I shared with my wife and moved into the senior apartment.\n- key: psychotic_symptoms_duration\n  name: Psychotic Symptoms Duration\n  description: 1 year and 1 month\n  manifestation: The suspicious thoughts about the apartment neighbors and office have been with me for about a year and a month.\n- key: depressive_episode_duration\n  name: Depressive Episode Duration\n  description: 1 year and 2 months\n  manifestation: The heavy, no-point feeling and pulling away from things have been going on for about a year and two months.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 59 / not_in_workforce / 9th_12th_no_diploma\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S019\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Have you generally preferred being alone over being with other people?\n\nPatient: Being alone? Yeah, mostly. I mean, I don't really go out of my way to be around crowds or anything. It's just easier that way, you know? Less hassle. I remember back when I was a kid, my old man would drag me to these massive family reunions out by the lake. Hundreds of people, cousins I didn't even know existed. Everyone was always yelling over each other, trying to get to the potato salad. My aunt Martha made the best potato salad, though. She put a little bit of mustard in it, gave it this real nice tang. You can't buy potato salad like that at the deli counter anymore. They always put too much mayonnaise in it now, makes it all soggy and heavy. I tried making it myself a few years back, bought the potatoes, the celery, the whole nine yards. But I ended up boiling the potatoes too long and it just turned into this big bowl of mush. So I just threw it out. But yeah, I guess I do like being by myself most of the time. Just me and my own space.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had unusual beliefs or experiences that other people would say are odd or hard to understand, even outside of this apartment situation?\n\nPatient: Unusual beliefs? No, I don't think so. I mean, I see things the way they are. People might think it's odd that I don't trust the water company, but that's just common sense. Have you seen what they put in the water these days? Fluoride, they say. Back in the day, you just drank from the tap and didn't think twice about it. I remember drinking straight from the garden hose in the summer when I was a kid. The water always tasted a little bit like rubber and dirt, but it was cold. We had this big green hose, must have been fifty feet long. My dad bought it at Sears. Sears used to be the place to go for everything. Tools, appliances, clothes. You could spend a whole Saturday just walking around Sears. I bought a socket set there in '82, Craftsman. Still have it. You can't beat that lifetime warranty. I tried to take a broken ratchet back to one of those new hardware stores last year, and the kid behind the counter looked at me like I had two heads. Didn't even know what a warranty was. Just told me I had to buy a new one. It's all about making a quick buck now. Nobody stands behind their products anymore. So, no, no unusual beliefs. Just... knowing how things used to be better, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Before this last year, did you tend to distrust or suspect other people’s motives for most of your adult life?\n\nPatient: Distrust people? Most of my life? No, I wouldn't say that. I mean, I've always just minded my own business, you know? Kept my head down. It's like when I used to work at the bottling plant back in the early nineties. You just did your shift and went home. The line boss there, this guy named Sal, he was always riding everybody. He had this little clipboard he carried around everywhere. Sal thought he was some big shot because he got to wear a white hard hat instead of a yellow one. I never understood why the color of a piece of plastic made someone think they were better than you. Hard hats are just to protect your skull, right? But Sal, he'd strut around... he drove this beat-up old Chevy Nova, faded blue, missing a hubcap on the back right tire. I always noticed that hubcap because I used to park my Ford Fairmont two spots down from him. That Fairmont was a good car. Had the bench seats in the front, the kind you don't see anymore. Everything's bucket seats now with that big center console taking up all the room. You can't even slide over to get out the passenger side if someone parks too close to you. It's ridiculous. But no, I didn't really distrust people before all this started. Just... you know, I just kept to myself mostly.\n\n[Last Question]\nOutside of the apartment building situation, do you often believe that other people are trying to deceive, harm, or take advantage of you?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Deceive or harm me? No, no, I wouldn't say that. Outside of the building, I mean, people are just people. They're mostly just trying to get through the day, same as anyone else. Like the checkout girl at the grocery store down the street, she's always ringing things up so fast. She wears these big hoop earrings, the silver kind. I always wonder if those get heavy pulling on your ears all day. My mother used to wear clip-on earrings. She had this whole jewelry box full of them, velvet-lined, smelled like old perfume and dust. She had this one pair that looked like little pearls, but they were just plastic. She wore them every Sunday to church. We went to this Methodist church on 4th Street, had these huge stained glass windows. The sun would come through them in the morning and turn the pews all blue and red. The pews were made of this really solid oak, polished so smooth you could slide right off if you weren't paying attention. They don't build churches like that anymore. Now they look like office buildings, all drywall and fluorescent lights. It just doesn't feel the same, you know? But no, I don't think people are trying to take advantage of me or anything. Not usually.

You can't buy potato salad like that at the deli counter anymore. They always put too much mayonnaise in it now, makes it all soggy and heavy. I tried making it myself a few years back, bought the potatoes, the celery, the whole nine yards. But I ended up boiling the potatoes too long and it just turned into this big bowl of mush. So I just threw it out. But yeah, I guess I do like being by myself most of the time. Just me and my own space.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had unusual beliefs or experiences that other people would say are odd or hard to understand, even outside of this apartment situation?\n\nPatient: Unusual beliefs? No, I don't think so. I mean, I see things the way they are. People might think it's odd that I don't trust the water company, but that's just common sense. Have you seen what they put in the water these days? Fluoride, they say. Back in the day, you just drank from the tap and didn't think twice about it. I remember drinking straight from the garden hose in the summer when I was a kid. The water always tasted a little bit like rubber and dirt, but it was cold. We had this big green hose, must have been fifty feet long. My dad bought it at Sears. Sears used to be the place to go for everything. Tools, appliances, clothes. You could spend a whole Saturday just walking around Sears. I bought a socket set there in '82, Craftsman. Still have it. You can't beat that lifetime warranty. I tried to take a broken ratchet back to one of those new hardware stores last year, and the kid behind the counter looked at me like I had two heads. Didn't even know what a warranty was. Just told me I had to buy a new one. It's all about making a quick buck now. Nobody stands behind their products anymore. So, no, no unusual beliefs. Just... knowing how things used to be better, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Before this last year, did you tend to distrust or suspect other people’s motives for most of your adult life?\n\nPatient: Distrust people? You just did your shift and went home. The line boss there, this guy named Sal, he was always riding everybody. He had this little clipboard he carried around everywhere. Sal thought he was some big shot because he got to wear a white hard hat instead of a yellow one. I never understood why the color of a piece of plastic made someone think they were better than you. Hard hats are just to protect your skull, right? But Sal, he'd strut around... he drove this beat-up old Chevy Nova, faded blue, missing a hubcap on the back right tire. I always noticed that hubcap because I used to park my Ford Fairmont two spots down from him. That Fairmont was a good car. Had the bench seats in the front, the kind you don't see anymore. But no, I didn't really distrust people before all this started. Just... you know, I just kept to myself mostly.\n\nDoctor: Outside of the apartment building situation, do you often believe that other people are trying to deceive, harm, or take advantage of you?\n\nPatient: Deceive or harm me? Unfaithful? No, no, I've never been married, so there was nobody to be unfaithful. I had a girlfriend once, a long time ago, Linda. She worked at the bakery downtown, the one that used to sell those big bear claws with the almond paste inside. I haven't had a good bear claw in years. The bakeries now, they just sell those dry scones that taste like cardboard. Anyway, Linda was a good girl, never gave me a reason to think she was doing anything wrong. 

But I didn't come in here to talk about Linda. I came in because... well, it's this apartment situation. I know the people in my building are watching my comings and goings. They keep track of when I leave and when I come back, and they're slipping notes about me to the management office. Everyone keeps telling me, "Oh, they're just routine notices on the doors, everybody gets them," but I know better. It's not just notices. They're keeping tabs on me. Just like how the mailman always seems to drop off the letters right when I'm walking out to get the paper. The paper used to be thicker, you know? The Sunday edition would take you all morning to read. Now it's barely a pamphlet. You can read the whole thing in ten minutes. They don't even have the good comic strips anymore. I used to love reading Peanuts. Good ol' Charlie Brown. He never could kick that football. Lucy always pulled it away at the last second. Just like people, I guess. Always pulling the rug out from under you.

Started back when I was working at the hardware store in '85. Anyway, yeah, they're watching me and slipping those notes.\n\nDoctor: Has this belief that people are watching you and leaving notes been present for at least one month?\n\nPatient: A month? No, no, it's been way longer than a month. It's been about a year and a month, actually. I know because it started right around the time I was trying to clean up the little patio area they give us. \n\nI had to go buy a new rake, and I walked down to that big box store on the highway. I usually hate those places. The aisles are too wide and you can never find anybody to help you. Back when I worked hardware, we actually talked to the customers, you know? We knew what we were talking about. Now it's just teenagers looking at their phones and self-checkout machines barking at you to bag your items. And the tools... don't get me started. I bought this plastic rake because it was all they had, and it was a complete piece of junk. Snapped on me in two weeks. You need solid wood and steel for a real tool. \n\nBut anyway, yeah, it was right around that time, a year and a month ago, that I first caught on to them watching me and slipping those notes around.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever heard voices that other people could not hear?\n\nPatient: Voices? No, I've never heard any voices. Well, except for the neighbors talking through the walls, but that's just because the walls are paper thin in that place. You can hear everything. I remember when I lived in this old brick building downtown back in the 90s, you couldn't hear a peep from anyone. Solid construction, you know? They don't build them like that anymore. Now it's all drywall and cheap insulation. I tried telling the landlord about it, but he just brushed me off. Said it was normal apartment living. Normal? Hah. I know what normal is, and having your neighbors sound like they're in your living room isn't it. I used to do some framing work myself, before the hardware store. We always made sure to use the right materials. But no, no voices that aren't there, just the real ones that are way too loud.\n\nDoctor: Have you generally been suspicious of other people or on guard about their motives for most of your adult life?\n\nPatient: Suspicious? Well, I mean, I guess I've always been a little careful, you know? You have to be. But not like this whole thing with the apartment building. That's different. But, you know, being on guard... it reminds me of this security guard we had at the hardware store back in '88. Guy's name was Frank. Frank used to walk the aisles like he was patrolling a military base. He had this big, heavy flashlight, the kind that takes like six D batteries. You don't see those much anymore, everything's LED now and rechargeable. Which is fine, I guess, but there was something about the weight of those old flashlights. You felt like you actually had something in your hand. Frank would shine that thing into every dark corner of the stockroom. He was always convinced kids were sneaking in to steal spray paint. Anyway, I wouldn't say I've been suspicious my whole life, no. Just... you know, paying attention to what's going on around me. Like Frank.\n\nDoctor: Have you generally preferred being alone over being with other people?\n\nPatient: Being alone? Yeah, mostly. I mean, I don't really go out of my way to be around crowds or anything. It's just easier that way, you know? Less hassle. I remember back when I was a kid, my old man would drag me to these massive family reunions out by the lake. Hundreds of people, cousins I didn't even know existed. Everyone was always yelling over each other, trying to get to the potato salad. My aunt Martha made the best potato salad, though. She put a little bit of mustard in it, gave it this real nice tang. You can't buy potato salad like that at the deli counter anymore. They always put too much mayonnaise in it now, makes it all soggy and heavy. I tried making it myself a few years back, bought the potatoes, the celery, the whole nine yards. But I ended up boiling the potatoes too long and it just turned into this big bowl of mush. So I just threw it out. But yeah, I guess I do like being by myself most of the time. Just me and my own space.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had unusual beliefs or experiences that other people would say are odd or hard to understand, even outside of this apartment situation?\n\nPatient: Unusual beliefs? No, I don't think so. I mean, I see things the way they are. People might think it's odd that I don't trust the water company, but that's just common sense. Have you seen what they put in the water these days? Fluoride, they say. Back in the day, you just drank from the tap and didn't think twice about it. I remember drinking straight from the garden hose in the summer when I was a kid. The water always tasted a little bit like rubber and dirt, but it was cold. We had this big green hose, must have been fifty feet long. My dad bought it at Sears. Sears used to be the place to go for everything. Tools, appliances, clothes. You could spend a whole Saturday just walking around Sears. I bought a socket set there in '82, Craftsman. Still have it. You can't beat that lifetime warranty. I tried to take a broken ratchet back to one of those new hardware stores last year, and the kid behind the counter looked at me like I had two heads. Didn't even know what a warranty was. Just told me I had to buy a new one. It's all about making a quick buck now. Nobody stands behind their products anymore. So, no, no unusual beliefs. Just... knowing how things used to be better, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Before this last year, did you tend to distrust or suspect other people’s motives for most of your adult life?\n\nPatient: Distrust people? Most of my life? No, I wouldn't say that. I mean, I've always just minded my own business, you know? Kept my head down. It's like when I used to work at the bottling plant back in the early nineties. You just did your shift and went home. The line boss there, this guy named Sal, he was always riding everybody. He had this little clipboard he carried around everywhere. Sal thought he was some big shot because he got to wear a white hard hat instead of a yellow one. I never understood why the color of a piece of plastic made someone think they were better than you. Hard hats are just to protect your skull, right? But Sal, he'd strut around... he drove this beat-up old Chevy Nova, faded blue, missing a hubcap on the back right tire. I always noticed that hubcap because I used to park my Ford Fairmont two spots down from him. That Fairmont was a good car. Had the bench seats in the front, the kind you don't see anymore. Everything's bucket seats now with that big center console taking up all the room. You can't even slide over to get out the passenger side if someone parks too close to you. It's ridiculous. But no, I didn't really distrust people before all this started. Just... you know, I just kept to myself mostly.\n\nDoctor: Outside of the apartment building situation, do you often believe that other people are trying to deceive, harm, or take advantage of you?\n\nPatient: Deceive or harm me? No, no, I wouldn't say that. Outside of the building, I mean, people are just people. They're mostly just trying to get through the day, same as anyone else. Like the checkout girl at the grocery store down the street, she's always ringing things up so fast. She wears these big hoop earrings, the silver kind. I always wonder if those get heavy pulling on your ears all day. My mother used to wear clip-on earrings. She had this whole jewelry box full of them, velvet-lined, smelled like old perfume and dust. She had this one pair that looked like little pearls, but they were just plastic. She wore them every Sunday to church. We went to this Methodist church on 4th Street, had these huge stained glass windows. The sun would come through them in the morning and turn the pews all blue and red. The pews were made of this really solid oak, polished so smooth you could slide right off if you weren't paying attention. They don't build churches like that anymore. Now they look like office buildings, all drywall and fluorescent lights. It just doesn't feel the same, you know? But no, I don't think people are trying to take advantage of me or anything. Not usually.\n\nDoctor: Before the apartment building situation started, did you ever have a strong, fixed belief that someone close to you was being unfaithful or deceiving you?\n\nPatient: Unfaithful? No, no, I've never been married, so there was nobody to be unfaithful. I had a girlfriend once, a long time ago, Linda. She worked at the bakery downtown, the one that used to sell those big bear claws with the almond paste inside. I haven't had a good bear claw in years. The bakeries now, they just sell those dry scones that taste like cardboard. Anyway, Linda was a good girl, never gave me a reason to think she was doing anything wrong. \n\nBut I didn't come in here to talk about Linda. I came in because... well, it's this apartment situation. I know the people in my building are watching my comings and goings. They keep track of when I leave and when I come back, and they're slipping notes about me to the management office. Everyone keeps telling me, \"Oh, they're just routine notices on the doors, everybody gets them,\" but I know better. It's not just notices. They're keeping tabs on me. Just like how the mailman always seems to drop off the letters right when I'm walking out to get the paper. The paper used to be thicker, you know? The Sunday edition would take you all morning to read. Now it's barely a pamphlet. You can read the whole thing in ten minutes. They don't even have the good comic strips anymore. I used to love reading Peanuts. Good ol' Charlie Brown. He never could kick that football. Lucy always pulled it away at the last second. Just like people, I guess. I always wonder if those get heavy pulling on your ears all day. My mother used to wear clip-on earrings. She had this whole jewelry box full of them, velvet-lined, smelled like old perfume and dust. She had this one pair that looked like little pearls, but they were just plastic. She wore them every Sunday to church. We went to this Methodist church on 4th Street, had these huge stained glass windows. The sun would come through them in the morning and turn the pews all blue and red. The pews were made of this really solid oak, polished so smooth you could slide right off if you weren't paying attention. They don't build churches like that anymore. Now they look like office buildings, all drywall and fluorescent lights. It just doesn't feel the same, you know? But no, I don't think people are trying to take advantage of me or anything. Not usually.\n\nDoctor: Before the apartment building situation started, did you ever have a strong, fixed belief that someone close to you was being unfaithful or deceiving you?\n\nPatient: Unfaithful? No, no, I've never been married, so there was nobody to be unfaithful. I had a girlfriend once, a long time ago, Linda. She worked at the bakery downtown, the one that used to sell those big bear claws with the almond paste inside. I haven't had a good bear claw in years. The bakeries now, they just sell those dry scones that taste like cardboard. Anyway, Linda was a good girl, never gave me a reason to think she was doing anything wrong. \n\nBut I didn't come in here to talk about Linda. I came in because... well, it's this apartment situation. I know the people in my building are watching my comings and goings. They keep track of when I leave and when I come back, and they're slipping notes about me to the management office. Everyone keeps telling me, \"Oh, they're just routine notices on the doors, everybody gets them,\" but I know better. It's not just notices. They're keeping tabs on me. Just like how the mailman always seems to drop off the letters right when I'm walking out to get the paper. The paper used to be thicker, you know? The Sunday edition would take you all morning to read. Now it's barely a pamphlet. You can read the whole thing in ten minutes. They don't even have the good comic strips anymore. I used to love reading Peanuts. Good ol' Charlie Brown. He never could kick that football. Lucy always pulled it away at the last second. Just like people, I guess. Always pulling the rug out from under you.\n\n[Last Question]\nBefore the apartment building situation started, did you usually have trouble trusting other people in general?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 59 / not_in_workforce / 9th_12th_no_diploma\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Trusting people in general? Before all this started? Well, I don't know, I guess I trusted people okay. I mean, you have to trust people a little bit, right? Like when you go to the diner, you trust the cook isn't going to spit in your food. I used to go to this diner on 5th and Main, Mel's Diner. Mel was this big guy, always wore a white apron that was never actually white by the end of the day. He made the best meatloaf sandwich. You can't find a good meatloaf sandwich anymore. Everybody wants fancy paninis with arugula and aioli. What even is aioli? It's just mayonnaise with garlic, if you ask me. But they charge you three extra dollars for it. Mel never charged extra for anything. He had this jukebox in the corner, played real records, not CDs or digital stuff. It cost a quarter to play three songs. I always played Johnny Cash. 'Ring of Fire,' usually. Cash had that deep voice, sounded like gravel. They don't make music like that anymore either. Now it's all synthesizers and computers making the beats. It's too loud, too. You go into a store now and you can't even hear yourself think over the music. But yeah, I guess I trusted people fine before. I just went about my business, you know? Never had much trouble with folks until this whole thing with the building started.

What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, I came in because... well, I know the people at my apartment building are watching my comings and goings. They're slipping notes about me to the office. Everybody keeps telling me it's just regular notices on the doors, like for maintenance or whatever, but I know what it really is. Speaking of maintenance, the guy who fixes the pipes, he came by last Tuesday. He had this wrench that looked like it belonged in a museum, I swear. I collect old tools, you know. Started back when I was working at the hardware store in '85. Anyway, yeah, they're watching me and slipping those notes.\n\nDoctor: Has this belief that people are watching you and leaving notes been present for at least one month?\n\nPatient: A month? No, no, it's been way longer than a month. It's been about a year and a month, actually. I know because it started right around the time I was trying to clean up the little patio area they give us. \n\nI had to go buy a new rake, and I walked down to that big box store on the highway. I usually hate those places. The aisles are too wide and you can never find anybody to help you. Back when I worked hardware, we actually talked to the customers, you know? We knew what we were talking about. Now it's just teenagers looking at their phones and self-checkout machines barking at you to bag your items. And the tools... don't get me started. I bought this plastic rake because it was all they had, and it was a complete piece of junk. Snapped on me in two weeks. You need solid wood and steel for a real tool. \n\nBut anyway, yeah, it was right around that time, a year and a month ago, that I first caught on to them watching me and slipping those notes around.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever heard voices that other people could not hear?\n\nPatient: Voices? No, I've never heard any voices. Well, except for the neighbors talking through the walls, but that's just because the walls are paper thin in that place. You can hear everything. I remember when I lived in this old brick building downtown back in the 90s, you couldn't hear a peep from anyone. Solid construction, you know? They don't build them like that anymore. Now it's all drywall and cheap insulation. I tried telling the landlord about it, but he just brushed me off. Said it was normal apartment living. Normal? Hah. I know what normal is, and having your neighbors sound like they're in your living room isn't it. I used to do some framing work myself, before the hardware store. We always made sure to use the right materials. But no, no voices that aren't there, just the real ones that are way too loud.\n\nDoctor: Have you generally been suspicious of other people or on guard about their motives for most of your adult life?\n\nPatient: Suspicious? Well, I mean, I guess I've always been a little careful, you know? You have to be. But not like this whole thing with the apartment building. That's different. But, you know, being on guard... it reminds me of this security guard we had at the hardware store back in '88. Guy's name was Frank. Frank used to walk the aisles like he was patrolling a military base. He had this big, heavy flashlight, the kind that takes like six D batteries. You don't see those much anymore, everything's LED now and rechargeable. Which is fine, I guess, but there was something about the weight of those old flashlights. You felt like you actually had something in your hand. Frank would shine that thing into every dark corner of the stockroom. He was always convinced kids were sneaking in to steal spray paint. Anyway, I wouldn't say I've been suspicious my whole life, no. Just... you know, paying attention to what's going on around me. Like Frank.\n\nDoctor: Have you generally preferred being alone over being with other people?\n\nPatient: Being alone? Yeah, mostly. I mean, I don't really go out of my way to be around crowds or anything. It's just easier that way, you know? Less hassle. I remember back when I was a kid, my old man would drag me to these massive family reunions out by the lake. Hundreds of people, cousins I didn't even know existed. Everyone was always yelling over each other, trying to get to the potato salad. My aunt Martha made the best potato salad, though. She put a little bit of mustard in it, gave it this real nice tang. You can't buy potato salad like that at the deli counter anymore. They always put too much mayonnaise in it now, makes it all soggy and heavy. I tried making it myself a few years back, bought the potatoes, the celery, the whole nine yards. But I ended up boiling the potatoes too long and it just turned into this big bowl of mush. So I just threw it out. But yeah, I guess I do like being by myself most of the time. Just me and my own space.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had unusual beliefs or experiences that other people would say are odd or hard to understand, even outside of this apartment situation?\n\nPatient: Unusual beliefs? No, I don't think so. I mean, I see things the way they are. People might think it's odd that I don't trust the water company, but that's just common sense. Have you seen what they put in the water these days? Fluoride, they say. Back in the day, you just drank from the tap and didn't think twice about it. I remember drinking straight from the garden hose in the summer when I was a kid. The water always tasted a little bit like rubber and dirt, but it was cold. We had this big green hose, must have been fifty feet long. My dad bought it at Sears. Sears used to be the place to go for everything. Tools, appliances, clothes. You could spend a whole Saturday just walking around Sears. I bought a socket set there in '82, Craftsman. Still have it. You can't beat that lifetime warranty. I tried to take a broken ratchet back to one of those new hardware stores last year, and the kid behind the counter looked at me like I had two heads. Didn't even know what a warranty was. Just told me I had to buy a new one. It's all about making a quick buck now. Nobody stands behind their products anymore. So, no, no unusual beliefs. Just... knowing how things used to be better, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Before this last year, did you tend to distrust or suspect other people’s motives for most of your adult life?\n\nPatient: Distrust people? Most of my life? No, I wouldn't say that. I mean, I've always just minded my own business, you know? Kept my head down. It's like when I used to work at the bottling plant back in the early nineties. You just did your shift and went home. The line boss there, this guy named Sal, he was always riding everybody. He had this little clipboard he carried around everywhere. Sal thought he was some big shot because he got to wear a white hard hat instead of a yellow one. I never understood why the color of a piece of plastic made someone think they were better than you. Hard hats are just to protect your skull, right? But Sal, he'd strut around... he drove this beat-up old Chevy Nova, faded blue, missing a hubcap on the back right tire. I always noticed that hubcap because I used to park my Ford Fairmont two spots down from him. That Fairmont was a good car. Had the bench seats in the front, the kind you don't see anymore. Everything's bucket seats now with that big center console taking up all the room. You can't even slide over to get out the passenger side if someone parks too close to you. It's ridiculous. But no, I didn't really distrust people before all this started. Just... you know, I just kept to myself mostly.\n\nDoctor: Outside of the apartment building situation, do you often believe that other people are trying to deceive, harm, or take advantage of you?\n\nPatient: Deceive or harm me? She had this one pair that looked like little pearls, but they were just plastic. She wore them every Sunday to church. Avoid things because of criticism? No, no, I wouldn't say that. I never really worried too much about what people thought of me, you know? I mean, I kept to myself mostly, sure. Never was one for big crowds or parties. Parties are always so loud anyway. People shouting over the music, trying to be heard. I remember going to a party once, must have been back in '85 or '86. It was my cousin Jimmy's housewarming. He bought this little split-level out in the suburbs. Had a built-in barbecue in the backyard, brick, really nice. He made these burgers, said they were his secret recipe. Turned out he just mixed onion soup mix into the ground beef. Everybody thought he was some kind of culinary genius. Onion soup mix! You can get a box of that for, what, a dollar? Used to be fifty cents. Everything's so expensive now. You go to the grocery store for a loaf of bread and a gallon of milk, and suddenly you're out twenty bucks. And the milk goes bad so fast now. I swear, they put something in it to make it spoil quicker so you have to buy more. But no, I never stayed home just because I thought people would reject me or anything like that. I just preferred my own company.

What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, I came in because... well, I know the people at my apartment building are watching my comings and goings. They're slipping notes about me to the office. Started back when I was working at the hardware store in '85. Anyway, yeah, they're watching me and slipping those notes.\n\nDoctor: Has this belief that people are watching you and leaving notes been present for at least one month?\n\nPatient: A month? No, no, it's been way longer than a month. It's been about a year and a month, actually. I know because it started right around the time I was trying to clean up the little patio area they give us. \n\nI had to go buy a new rake, and I walked down to that big box store on the highway. I usually hate those places. The aisles are too wide and you can never find anybody to help you. Back when I worked hardware, we actually talked to the customers, you know? We knew what we were talking about. Now it's just teenagers looking at their phones and self-checkout machines barking at you to bag your items. And the tools... don't get me started. I bought this plastic rake because it was all they had, and it was a complete piece of junk. Snapped on me in two weeks. You need solid wood and steel for a real tool. \n\nBut anyway, yeah, it was right around that time, a year and a month ago, that I first caught on to them watching me and slipping those notes around.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever heard voices that other people could not hear?\n\nPatient: Voices? No, I've never heard any voices. Well, except for the neighbors talking through the walls, but that's just because the walls are paper thin in that place. You can hear everything. I remember when I lived in this old brick building downtown back in the 90s, you couldn't hear a peep from anyone. Solid construction, you know? They don't build them like that anymore. Now it's all drywall and cheap insulation. I tried telling the landlord about it, but he just brushed me off. Said it was normal apartment living. Normal? Hah. I know what normal is, and having your neighbors sound like they're in your living room isn't it. I used to do some framing work myself, before the hardware store. We always made sure to use the right materials. But no, no voices that aren't there, just the real ones that are way too loud.\n\nDoctor: Have you generally been suspicious of other people or on guard about their motives for most of your adult life?\n\nPatient: Suspicious? Well, I mean, I guess I've always been a little careful, you know? You have to be. But not like this whole thing with the apartment building. That's different. But, you know, being on guard... it reminds me of this security guard we had at the hardware store back in '88. Guy's name was Frank. Frank used to walk the aisles like he was patrolling a military base. He had this big, heavy flashlight, the kind that takes like six D batteries. You don't see those much anymore, everything's LED now and rechargeable. Which is fine, I guess, but there was something about the weight of those old flashlights. You felt like you actually had something in your hand. Frank would shine that thing into every dark corner of the stockroom. He was always convinced kids were sneaking in to steal spray paint. Anyway, I wouldn't say I've been suspicious my whole life, no. Just... you know, paying attention to what's going on around me. Like Frank.\n\nDoctor: Have you generally preferred being alone over being with other people?\n\nPatient: Being alone? Yeah, mostly. I mean, I don't really go out of my way to be around crowds or anything. It's just easier that way, you know? Less hassle. I remember back when I was a kid, my old man would drag me to these massive family reunions out by the lake. Hundreds of people, cousins I didn't even know existed. Everyone was always yelling over each other, trying to get to the potato salad. My aunt Martha made the best potato salad, though. She put a little bit of mustard in it, gave it this real nice tang. You can't buy potato salad like that at the deli counter anymore. They always put too much mayonnaise in it now, makes it all soggy and heavy. I tried making it myself a few years back, bought the potatoes, the celery, the whole nine yards. But I ended up boiling the potatoes too long and it just turned into this big bowl of mush. So I just threw it out. But yeah, I guess I do like being by myself most of the time. Just me and my own space.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had unusual beliefs or experiences that other people would say are odd or hard to understand, even outside of this apartment situation?\n\nPatient: Unusual beliefs? No, I don't think so. I mean, I see things the way they are. People might think it's odd that I don't trust the water company, but that's just common sense. Have you seen what they put in the water these days? Fluoride, they say. Back in the day, you just drank from the tap and didn't think twice about it. I remember drinking straight from the garden hose in the summer when I was a kid. The water always tasted a little bit like rubber and dirt, but it was cold. We had this big green hose, must have been fifty feet long. My dad bought it at Sears. Sears used to be the place to go for everything. Tools, appliances, clothes. You could spend a whole Saturday just walking around Sears. I bought a socket set there in '82, Craftsman. Still have it. You can't beat that lifetime warranty. I tried to take a broken ratchet back to one of those new hardware stores last year, and the kid behind the counter looked at me like I had two heads. Didn't even know what a warranty was. Just told me I had to buy a new one. It's all about making a quick buck now. Nobody stands behind their products anymore. So, no, no unusual beliefs. Just... knowing how things used to be better, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Before this last year, did you tend to distrust or suspect other people’s motives for most of your adult life?\n\nPatient: Distrust people? Most of my life? No, I wouldn't say that. I mean, I've always just minded my own business, you know? Kept my head down. It's like when I used to work at the bottling plant back in the early nineties. You just did your shift and went home. The line boss there, this guy named Sal, he was always riding everybody. He had this little clipboard he carried around everywhere. Sal thought he was some big shot because he got to wear a white hard hat instead of a yellow one. I never understood why the color of a piece of plastic made someone think they were better than you. Hard hats are just to protect your skull, right? But Sal, he'd strut around... he drove this beat-up old Chevy Nova, faded blue, missing a hubcap on the back right tire. I always noticed that hubcap because I used to park my Ford Fairmont two spots down from him. That Fairmont was a good car. Had the bench seats in the front, the kind you don't see anymore. Everything's bucket seats now with that big center console taking up all the room. You can't even slide over to get out the passenger side if someone parks too close to you. It's ridiculous. The Sunday edition would take you all morning to read. Now it's barely a pamphlet. You can read the whole thing in ten minutes. They don't even have the good comic strips anymore. I used to love reading Peanuts. Good ol' Charlie Brown. He never could kick that football. Lucy always pulled it away at the last second. Just like people, I guess. Always pulling the rug out from under you.\n\nDoctor: Before the apartment building situation started, did you usually have trouble trusting other people in general?\n\nPatient: Trusting people in general? Before all this started? Well, I don't know, I guess I trusted people okay. I mean, you have to trust people a little bit, right? Like when you go to the diner, you trust the cook isn't going to spit in your food. I used to go to this diner on 5th and Main, Mel's Diner. Mel was this big guy, always wore a white apron that was never actually white by the end of the day. He made the best meatloaf sandwich. You can't find a good meatloaf sandwich anymore. Everybody wants fancy paninis with arugula and aioli. What even is aioli? It's just mayonnaise with garlic, if you ask me. But they charge you three extra dollars for it. Mel never charged extra for anything. He had this jukebox in the corner, played real records, not CDs or digital stuff. It cost a quarter to play three songs. I always played Johnny Cash. 'Ring of Fire,' usually. Cash had that deep voice, sounded like gravel. They don't make music like that anymore either. Now it's all synthesizers and computers making the beats. It's too loud, too. You go into a store now and you can't even hear yourself think over the music. But yeah, I guess I trusted people fine before. I just went about my business, you know? Never had much trouble with folks until this whole thing with the building started.\n\nDoctor: Before the apartment building situation started, did you usually avoid social or work activities because you feared criticism or rejection from other people?\n\nPatient: Avoid things because of criticism? No, no, I wouldn't say that. I never really worried too much about what people thought of me, you know? I mean, I kept to myself mostly, sure. Never was one for big crowds or parties. Parties are always so loud anyway. People shouting over the music, trying to be heard. I remember going to a party once, must have been back in '85 or '86. It was my cousin Jimmy's housewarming. He bought this little split-level out in the suburbs. Had a built-in barbecue in the backyard, brick, really nice. He made these burgers, said they were his secret recipe. Turned out he just mixed onion soup mix into the ground beef. Everybody thought he was some kind of culinary genius. Onion soup mix! You can get a box of that for, what, a dollar? Just like how the mailman always seems to drop off the letters right when I'm walking out to get the paper. The paper used to be thicker, you know? The Sunday edition would take you all morning to read. Now it's barely a pamphlet. You can read the whole thing in ten minutes. They don't even have the good comic strips anymore. I used to love reading Peanuts. Good ol' Charlie Brown. He never could kick that football. Lucy always pulled it away at the last second. Just like people, I guess. Always pulling the rug out from under you.\n\nDoctor: Before the apartment building situation started, did you usually have trouble trusting other people in general?\n\nPatient: Trusting people in general? Before all this started? Well, I don't know, I guess I trusted people okay. I mean, you have to trust people a little bit, right? Like when you go to the diner, you trust the cook isn't going to spit in your food. I used to go to this diner on 5th and Main, Mel's Diner. Mel was this big guy, always wore a white apron that was never actually white by the end of the day. He made the best meatloaf sandwich. You can't find a good meatloaf sandwich anymore. Everybody wants fancy paninis with arugula and aioli. What even is aioli? It's just mayonnaise with garlic, if you ask me. But they charge you three extra dollars for it. Mel never charged extra for anything. He had this jukebox in the corner, played real records, not CDs or digital stuff. It cost a quarter to play three songs. I always played Johnny Cash. 'Ring of Fire,' usually. Cash had that deep voice, sounded like gravel. They don't make music like that anymore either. Now it's all synthesizers and computers making the beats. It's too loud, too. You go into a store now and you can't even hear yourself think over the music. But yeah, I guess I trusted people fine before. I just went about my business, you know? Never had much trouble with folks until this whole thing with the building started.\n\nDoctor: Before the apartment building situation started, did you usually avoid social or work activities because you feared criticism or rejection from other people?\n\nPatient: Avoid things because of criticism? No, no, I wouldn't say that. I never really worried too much about what people thought of me, you know? I mean, I kept to myself mostly, sure. Never was one for big crowds or parties. Parties are always so loud anyway. People shouting over the music, trying to be heard. I remember going to a party once, must have been back in '85 or '86. It was my cousin Jimmy's housewarming. He bought this little split-level out in the suburbs. Had a built-in barbecue in the backyard, brick, really nice. He made these burgers, said they were his secret recipe. Turned out he just mixed onion soup mix into the ground beef. Everybody thought he was some kind of culinary genius. Onion soup mix! You can get a box of that for, what, a dollar? Used to be fifty cents. Everything's so expensive now. You go to the grocery store for a loaf of bread and a gallon of milk, and suddenly you're out twenty bucks. And the milk goes bad so fast now. I swear, they put something in it to make it spoil quicker so you have to buy more. But no, I never stayed home just because I thought people would reject me or anything like that. I just preferred my own company.\n\n[Last Question]\nOutside of the apartment building issue, do you often find yourself suspicious of different people in many parts of your life?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 59 / not_in_workforce / 9th_12th_no_diploma\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Suspicious of different people? No, not really. I mean, I don't go around thinking everybody's out to get me, if that's what you mean. It's just the folks in my building. Outside of that, I don't pay much mind to strangers. Like when I take the bus. I take the 42 down to the pharmacy sometimes. The buses used to run right on time, you could set your watch by them. Now you're waiting twenty, thirty minutes in the freezing cold. And the seats, they have that weird fuzzy fabric with the crazy patterns on them. I read somewhere they design it like that so you can't see how dirty it is. Makes you wonder when they actually wash them. Probably never. My mother used to wash our slipcovers every spring. Had this big wringer washer in the basement. You had to be careful or you'd get your fingers caught in the rollers. Hurt like the dickens. She'd hang them on the line out back, and they'd smell like fresh air and sunshine. You can't get that smell from a bottle of detergent, no matter what the commercials say. They put all those chemicals in the soap now, gives people rashes. But anyway, no, I'm not suspicious of the bus driver or the lady at the pharmacy or anything. It's just my building.

  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n12\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF22, F60.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, I’m glad you came in. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, I came in because... well, I know the people at my apartment building are watching my comings and goings. They're slipping notes about me to the office. Everybody keeps telling me it's just regular notices on the doors, like for maintenance or whatever, but I know what it really is. Speaking of maintenance, the guy who fixes the pipes, he came by last Tuesday. He had this wrench that looked like it belonged in a museum, I swear. I collect old tools, you know. Started back when I was working at the hardware store in '85. Anyway, yeah, they're watching me and slipping those notes.\n\nDoctor: Has this belief that people are watching you and leaving notes been present for at least one month?\n\nPatient: A month? No, no, it's been way longer than a month. It's been about a year and a month, actually. I know because it started right around the time I was trying to clean up the little patio area they give us. \n\nI had to go buy a new rake, and I walked down to that big box store on the highway. I usually hate those places. The aisles are too wide and you can never find anybody to help you. Back when I worked hardware, we actually talked to the customers, you know? We knew what we were talking about. Now it's just teenagers looking at their phones and self-checkout machines barking at you to bag your items. And the tools... don't get me started. I bought this plastic rake because it was all they had, and it was a complete piece of junk. Snapped on me in two weeks. You need solid wood and steel for a real tool. \n\nBut anyway, yeah, it was right around that time, a year and a month ago, that I first caught on to them watching me and slipping those notes around.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever heard voices that other people could not hear?\n\nPatient: Voices? No, I've never heard any voices. Well, except for the neighbors talking through the walls, but that's just because the walls are paper thin in that place. You can hear everything. I remember when I lived in this old brick building downtown back in the 90s, you couldn't hear a peep from anyone. Solid construction, you know? They don't build them like that anymore. Now it's all drywall and cheap insulation. I tried telling the landlord about it, but he just brushed me off. Said it was normal apartment living. Normal? Hah. I know what normal is, and having your neighbors sound like they're in your living room isn't it. I used to do some framing work myself, before the hardware store. We always made sure to use the right materials. But no, no voices that aren't there, just the real ones that are way too loud.\n\nDoctor: Have you generally been suspicious of other people or on guard about their motives for most of your adult life?\n\nPatient: Suspicious? Well, I mean, I guess I've always been a little careful, you know? You have to be. But not like this whole thing with the apartment building. That's different. But, you know, being on guard... it reminds me of this security guard we had at the hardware store back in '88. Guy's name was Frank. Frank used to walk the aisles like he was patrolling a military base. He had this big, heavy flashlight, the kind that takes like six D batteries. You don't see those much anymore, everything's LED now and rechargeable. Which is fine, I guess, but there was something about the weight of those old flashlights. You felt like you actually had something in your hand. Frank would shine that thing into every dark corner of the stockroom. He was always convinced kids were sneaking in to steal spray paint. Anyway, I wouldn't say I've been suspicious my whole life, no. Just... you know, paying attention to what's going on around me. Like Frank.\n\nDoctor: Have you generally preferred being alone over being with other people?\n\nPatient: Being alone? Yeah, mostly. I mean, I don't really go out of my way to be around crowds or anything. It's just easier that way, you know? Less hassle. I remember back when I was a kid, my old man would drag me to these massive family reunions out by the lake. Hundreds of people, cousins I didn't even know existed. Everyone was always yelling over each other, trying to get to the potato salad. My aunt Martha made the best potato salad, though. She put a little bit of mustard in it, gave it this real nice tang. You can't buy potato salad like that at the deli counter anymore. They always put too much mayonnaise in it now, makes it all soggy and heavy. I tried making it myself a few years back, bought the potatoes, the celery, the whole nine yards. But I ended up boiling the potatoes too long and it just turned into this big bowl of mush. So I just threw it out. But yeah, I guess I do like being by myself most of the time. Just me and my own space.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had unusual beliefs or experiences that other people would say are odd or hard to understand, even outside of this apartment situation?\n\nPatient: Unusual beliefs? No, I don't think so. I mean, I see things the way they are. People might think it's odd that I don't trust the water company, but that's just common sense. Have you seen what they put in the water these days? Fluoride, they say. Back in the day, you just drank from the tap and didn't think twice about it. I remember drinking straight from the garden hose in the summer when I was a kid. The water always tasted a little bit like rubber and dirt, but it was cold. We had this big green hose, must have been fifty feet long. My dad bought it at Sears. Sears used to be the place to go for everything. Tools, appliances, clothes. You could spend a whole Saturday just walking around Sears. I bought a socket set there in '82, Craftsman. Still have it. You can't beat that lifetime warranty. I tried to take a broken ratchet back to one of those new hardware stores last year, and the kid behind the counter looked at me like I had two heads. Didn't even know what a warranty was. Just told me I had to buy a new one. It's all about making a quick buck now. Nobody stands behind their products anymore. So, no, no unusual beliefs. Just... knowing how things used to be better, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Before this last year, did you tend to distrust or suspect other people’s motives for most of your adult life?\n\nPatient: Distrust people? Most of my life? No, I wouldn't say that. I mean, I've always just minded my own business, you know? Kept my head down. It's like when I used to work at the bottling plant back in the early nineties. You just did your shift and went home. The line boss there, this guy named Sal, he was always riding everybody. He had this little clipboard he carried around everywhere. Sal thought he was some big shot because he got to wear a white hard hat instead of a yellow one. I never understood why the color of a piece of plastic made someone think they were better than you. Hard hats are just to protect your skull, right? But Sal, he'd strut around... he drove this beat-up old Chevy Nova, faded blue, missing a hubcap on the back right tire. I always noticed that hubcap because I used to park my Ford Fairmont two spots down from him. That Fairmont was a good car. Had the bench seats in the front, the kind you don't see anymore. Everything's bucket seats now with that big center console taking up all the room. You can't even slide over to get out the passenger side if someone parks too close to you. It's ridiculous. But no, I didn't really distrust people before all this started. Just... you know, I just kept to myself mostly.\n\nDoctor: Outside of the apartment building situation, do you often believe that other people are trying to deceive, harm, or take advantage of you?\n\nPatient: Deceive or harm me? No, no, I wouldn't say that. Outside of the building, I mean, people are just people. They're mostly just trying to get through the day, same as anyone else. Like the checkout girl at the grocery store down the street, she's always ringing things up so fast. She wears these big hoop earrings, the silver kind. I always wonder if those get heavy pulling on your ears all day. My mother used to wear clip-on earrings. She had this whole jewelry box full of them, velvet-lined, smelled like old perfume and dust. She had this one pair that looked like little pearls, but they were just plastic. She wore them every Sunday to church. We went to this Methodist church on 4th Street, had these huge stained glass windows. The sun would come through them in the morning and turn the pews all blue and red. The pews were made of this really solid oak, polished so smooth you could slide right off if you weren't paying attention. They don't build churches like that anymore. Now they look like office buildings, all drywall and fluorescent lights. It just doesn't feel the same, you know? But no, I don't think people are trying to take advantage of me or anything. Not usually.\n\nDoctor: Before the apartment building situation started, did you ever have a strong, fixed belief that someone close to you was being unfaithful or deceiving you?\n\nPatient: Unfaithful? No, no, I've never been married, so there was nobody to be unfaithful. I had a girlfriend once, a long time ago, Linda. She worked at the bakery downtown, the one that used to sell those big bear claws with the almond paste inside. I haven't had a good bear claw in years. The bakeries now, they just sell those dry scones that taste like cardboard. Anyway, Linda was a good girl, never gave me a reason to think she was doing anything wrong. \n\nBut I didn't come in here to talk about Linda. I came in because... well, it's this apartment situation. I know the people in my building are watching my comings and goings. They keep track of when I leave and when I come back, and they're slipping notes about me to the management office. Everyone keeps telling me, \"Oh, they're just routine notices on the doors, everybody gets them,\" but I know better. It's not just notices. They're keeping tabs on me. Just like how the mailman always seems to drop off the letters right when I'm walking out to get the paper. The paper used to be thicker, you know? The Sunday edition would take you all morning to read. Now it's barely a pamphlet. You can read the whole thing in ten minutes. They don't even have the good comic strips anymore. I used to love reading Peanuts. Good ol' Charlie Brown. He never could kick that football. Lucy always pulled it away at the last second. Just like people, I guess. Always pulling the rug out from under you.\n\nDoctor: Before the apartment building situation started, did you usually have trouble trusting other people in general?\n\nPatient: Trusting people in general? Before all this started? Well, I don't know, I guess I trusted people okay. I mean, you have to trust people a little bit, right? Like when you go to the diner, you trust the cook isn't going to spit in your food. I used to go to this diner on 5th and Main, Mel's Diner. Mel was this big guy, always wore a white apron that was never actually white by the end of the day. He made the best meatloaf sandwich. You can't find a good meatloaf sandwich anymore. Everybody wants fancy paninis with arugula and aioli. What even is aioli? It's just mayonnaise with garlic, if you ask me. But they charge you three extra dollars for it. No, no, I wouldn't say that. I never really worried too much about what people thought of me, you know? I mean, I kept to myself mostly, sure. Never was one for big crowds or parties. Parties are always so loud anyway. People shouting over the music, trying to be heard. I remember going to a party once, must have been back in '85 or '86. It was my cousin Jimmy's housewarming. Turned out he just mixed onion soup mix into the ground beef. Everybody thought he was some kind of culinary genius. Onion soup mix! You can get a box of that for, what, a dollar?
