Now they look like office buildings, all drywall and fluorescent lights. It just doesn't feel the same, you know? But no, I don't think people are trying to take advantage of me or anything. Not usually.\n\nDoctor: Before the apartment building situation started, did you ever have a strong, fixed belief that someone close to you was being unfaithful or deceiving you?\n\nPatient: Unfaithful? No, no, I've never been married, so there was nobody to be unfaithful. I had a girlfriend once, a long time ago, Linda. She worked at the bakery downtown, the one that used to sell those big bear claws with the almond paste inside. I haven't had a good bear claw in years. The bakeries now, they just sell those dry scones that taste like cardboard. Anyway, Linda was a good girl, never gave me a reason to think she was doing anything wrong. \n\nBut I didn't come in here to talk about Linda. I came in because... well, it's this apartment situation. I know the people in my building are watching my comings and goings. They keep track of when I leave and when I come back, and they're slipping notes about me to the management office. Everyone keeps telling me, \"Oh, they're just routine notices on the doors, everybody gets them,\" but I know better. It's not just notices. They're keeping tabs on me. Just like how the mailman always seems to drop off the letters right when I'm walking out to get the paper. The paper used to be thicker, you know? The Sunday edition would take you all morning to read. Now it's barely a pamphlet. You can read the whole thing in ten minutes. They don't even have the good comic strips anymore. I used to love reading Peanuts. Good ol' Charlie Brown. He never could kick that football. Lucy always pulled it away at the last second. Just like people, I guess. Always pulling the rug out from under you.\n\nDoctor: Before the apartment building situation started, did you usually have trouble trusting other people in general?\n\nPatient: Trusting people in general? Before all this started? Well, I don't know, I guess I trusted people okay. I mean, you have to trust people a little bit, right? Like when you go to the diner, you trust the cook isn't going to spit in your food. I used to go to this diner on 5th and Main, Mel's Diner. Mel was this big guy, always wore a white apron that was never actually white by the end of the day. He made the best meatloaf sandwich. You can't find a good meatloaf sandwich anymore. Everybody wants fancy paninis with arugula and aioli. What even is aioli? It's just mayonnaise with garlic, if you ask me. But they charge you three extra dollars for it. Mel never charged extra for anything. He had this jukebox in the corner, played real records, not CDs or digital stuff. It cost a quarter to play three songs. I always played Johnny Cash. 'Ring of Fire,' usually. Cash had that deep voice, sounded like gravel. They don't make music like that anymore either. Now it's all synthesizers and computers making the beats. It's too loud, too. You go into a store now and you can't even hear yourself think over the music. But yeah, I guess I trusted people fine before. I just went about my business, you know? Never had much trouble with folks until this whole thing with the building started.\n\nDoctor: Before the apartment building situation started, did you usually avoid social or work activities because you feared criticism or rejection from other people?\n\nPatient: Avoid things because of criticism? No, no, I wouldn't say that. I never really worried too much about what people thought of me, you know? I mean, I kept to myself mostly, sure. Never was one for big crowds or parties. Parties are always so loud anyway. People shouting over the music, trying to be heard. I remember going to a party once, must have been back in '85 or '86. It was my cousin Jimmy's housewarming. He bought this little split-level out in the suburbs. Had a built-in barbecue in the backyard, brick, really nice. He made these burgers, said they were his secret recipe. Turned out he just mixed onion soup mix into the ground beef. Everybody thought he was some kind of culinary genius. Onion soup mix! You can get a box of that for, what, a dollar? Used to be fifty cents. Everything's so expensive now. You go to the grocery store for a loaf of bread and a gallon of milk, and suddenly you're out twenty bucks. And the milk goes bad so fast now. I swear, they put something in it to make it spoil quicker so you have to buy more. But no, I never stayed home just because I thought people would reject me or anything like that. I just preferred my own company.\n\nDoctor: Outside of the apartment building issue, do you often find yourself suspicious of different people in many parts of your life?\n\nPatient: Suspicious of different people? No, not really. I mean, I don't go around thinking everybody's out to get me, if that's what you mean. It's just the folks in my building. Outside of that, I don't pay much mind to strangers. Like when I take the bus. I take the 42 down to the pharmacy sometimes. The buses used to run right on time, you could set your watch by them. Now you're waiting twenty, thirty minutes in the freezing cold. And the seats, they have that weird fuzzy fabric with the crazy patterns on them. I read somewhere they design it like that so you can't see how dirty it is. Makes you wonder when they actually wash them. Probably never. My mother used to wash our slipcovers every spring. Had this big wringer washer in the basement. You had to be careful or you'd get your fingers caught in the rollers. Hurt like the dickens. She'd hang them on the line out back, and they'd smell like fresh air and sunshine. You can't get that smell from a bottle of detergent, no matter what the commercials say. They put all those chemicals in the soap now, gives people rashes. But anyway, no, I'm not suspicious of the bus driver or the lady at the pharmacy or anything. It's just my building.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Hidden messages? No, I don't think so. I mean, people talk, sure, but I don't think they're talking about me or trying to send me secret codes or anything. Well, except for the building, like I said. But everywhere else? No. I just watch the news sometimes, the local channel. They always have the same anchors, the guy with the hair that doesn't move and the lady who smiles too much even when she's talking about a house fire. It's unsettling, really. How can you smile about a fire? I knew a guy who lost his house in a fire back in the nineties. Electrical fault, they said. It was one of those old Victorian houses, beautiful woodwork, all gone in a couple of hours. He had this collection of antique clocks, grandfather clocks, cuckoo clocks, you name it. The heat just melted them all together into one big lump of brass and wood. It's a shame. You can't replace history like that. Now they build houses out of cheap pine and drywall, they go up like matchsticks. But no, the news anchors aren't sending me messages. They just read off the teleprompter. You can see their eyes moving back and forth if you look close enough.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF22, F60.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, I’m glad you came in. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, I came in because... well, I know the people at my apartment building are watching my comings and goings. They're slipping notes about me to the office. Everybody keeps telling me it's just regular notices on the doors, like for maintenance or whatever, but I know what it really is. Speaking of maintenance, the guy who fixes the pipes, he came by last Tuesday. He had this wrench that looked like it belonged in a museum, I swear. I collect old tools, you know. Started back when I was working at the hardware store in '85. Anyway, yeah, they're watching me and slipping those notes.\n\nDoctor: Has this belief that people are watching you and leaving notes been present for at least one month?\n\nPatient: A month? No, no, it's been way longer than a month. It's been about a year and a month, actually. I know because it started right around the time I was trying to clean up the little patio area they give us. \n\nI had to go buy a new rake, and I walked down to that big box store on the highway. I usually hate those places. The aisles are too wide and you can never find anybody to help you. Back when I worked hardware, we actually talked to the customers, you know? We knew what we were talking about. Now it's just teenagers looking at their phones and self-checkout machines barking at you to bag your items. And the tools... don't get me started. I bought this plastic rake because it was all they had, and it was a complete piece of junk. Snapped on me in two weeks. You need solid wood and steel for a real tool. \n\nBut anyway, yeah, it was right around that time, a year and a month ago, that I first caught on to them watching me and slipping those notes around.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever heard voices that other people could not hear?\n\nPatient: Voices? No, I've never heard any voices. Well, except for the neighbors talking through the walls, but that's just because the walls are paper thin in that place. You can hear everything. I remember when I lived in this old brick building downtown back in the 90s, you couldn't hear a peep from anyone. Solid construction, you know? They don't build them like that anymore. Now it's all drywall and cheap insulation. I tried telling the landlord about it, but he just brushed me off. Said it was normal apartment living. Normal? Hah. I know what normal is, and having your neighbors sound like they're in your living room isn't it. I used to do some framing work myself, before the hardware store. We always made sure to use the right materials. But no, no voices that aren't there, just the real ones that are way too loud.\n\nDoctor: Have you generally been suspicious of other people or on guard about their motives for most of your adult life?\n\nPatient: Suspicious? Well, I mean, I guess I've always been a little careful, you know? You have to be. But not like this whole thing with the apartment building. That's different. But, you know, being on guard... it reminds me of this security guard we had at the hardware store back in '88. Guy's name was Frank. Frank used to walk the aisles like he was patrolling a military base. He had this big, heavy flashlight, the kind that takes like six D batteries. You don't see those much anymore, everything's LED now and rechargeable. Which is fine, I guess, but there was something about the weight of those old flashlights. You felt like you actually had something in your hand. Frank would shine that thing into every dark corner of the stockroom. He was always convinced kids were sneaking in to steal spray paint. Anyway, I wouldn't say I've been suspicious my whole life, no. Just... you know, paying attention to what's going on around me. Like Frank.\n\nDoctor: Have you generally preferred being alone over being with other people?\n\nPatient: Being alone? Yeah, mostly. I mean, I don't really go out of my way to be around crowds or anything. It's just easier that way, you know? Less hassle. I remember back when I was a kid, my old man would drag me to these massive family reunions out by the lake. Hundreds of people, cousins I didn't even know existed. Everyone was always yelling over each other, trying to get to the potato salad. My aunt Martha made the best potato salad, though. She put a little bit of mustard in it, gave it this real nice tang. You can't buy potato salad like that at the deli counter anymore. They always put too much mayonnaise in it now, makes it all soggy and heavy. I tried making it myself a few years back, bought the potatoes, the celery, the whole nine yards. But I ended up boiling the potatoes too long and it just turned into this big bowl of mush. So I just threw it out. But yeah, I guess I do like being by myself most of the time. Just me and my own space.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had unusual beliefs or experiences that other people would say are odd or hard to understand, even outside of this apartment situation?\n\nPatient: Unusual beliefs? No, I don't think so. I mean, I see things the way they are. People might think it's odd that I don't trust the water company, but that's just common sense. Have you seen what they put in the water these days? Fluoride, they say. Back in the day, you just drank from the tap and didn't think twice about it. I remember drinking straight from the garden hose in the summer when I was a kid. The water always tasted a little bit like rubber and dirt, but it was cold. We had this big green hose, must have been fifty feet long. My dad bought it at Sears. Sears used to be the place to go for everything. Tools, appliances, clothes. You could spend a whole Saturday just walking around Sears. I bought a socket set there in '82, Craftsman. Still have it. You can't beat that lifetime warranty. I tried to take a broken ratchet back to one of those new hardware stores last year, and the kid behind the counter looked at me like I had two heads. Didn't even know what a warranty was. Just told me I had to buy a new one. It's all about making a quick buck now. Nobody stands behind their products anymore. So, no, no unusual beliefs. Just... knowing how things used to be better, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Before this last year, did you tend to distrust or suspect other people’s motives for most of your adult life?\n\nPatient: Distrust people? Most of my life? No, I wouldn't say that. I mean, I've always just minded my own business, you know? Kept my head down. It's like when I used to work at the bottling plant back in the early nineties. You just did your shift and went home. The line boss there, this guy named Sal, he was always riding everybody. He had this little clipboard he carried around everywhere. Sal thought he was some big shot because he got to wear a white hard hat instead of a yellow one. I never understood why the color of a piece of plastic made someone think they were better than you. Hard hats are just to protect your skull, right? But Sal, he'd strut around... he drove this beat-up old Chevy Nova, faded blue, missing a hubcap on the back right tire. I always noticed that hubcap because I used to park my Ford Fairmont two spots down from him. That Fairmont was a good car. Had the bench seats in the front, the kind you don't see anymore. Everything's bucket seats now with that big center console taking up all the room. You can't even slide over to get out the passenger side if someone parks too close to you. It's ridiculous. But no, I didn't really distrust people before all this started. Just... you know, I just kept to myself mostly.\n\nDoctor: Outside of the apartment building situation, do you often believe that other people are trying to deceive, harm, or take advantage of you?\n\nPatient: Deceive or harm me? No, no, I wouldn't say that. Outside of the building, I mean, people are just people. They're mostly just trying to get through the day, same as anyone else. Like the checkout girl at the grocery store down the street, she's always ringing things up so fast. She wears these big hoop earrings, the silver kind. I always wonder if those get heavy pulling on your ears all day. My mother used to wear clip-on earrings. She had this whole jewelry box full of them, velvet-lined, smelled like old perfume and dust. She had this one pair that looked like little pearls, but they were just plastic. She wore them every Sunday to church. We went to this Methodist church on 4th Street, had these huge stained glass windows. The sun would come through them in the morning and turn the pews all blue and red. The pews were made of this really solid oak, polished so smooth you could slide right off if you weren't paying attention. They don't build churches like that anymore. Now they look like office buildings, all drywall and fluorescent lights. It just doesn't feel the same, you know? But no, I don't think people are trying to take advantage of me or anything. Not usually.\n\nDoctor: Before the apartment building situation started, did you ever have a strong, fixed belief that someone close to you was being unfaithful or deceiving you?\n\nPatient: Unfaithful? No, no, I've never been married, so there was nobody to be unfaithful. I had a girlfriend once, a long time ago, Linda. She worked at the bakery downtown, the one that used to sell those big bear claws with the almond paste inside. I haven't had a good bear claw in years. The bakeries now, they just sell those dry scones that taste like cardboard. Anyway, Linda was a good girl, never gave me a reason to think she was doing anything wrong. \n\nBut I didn't come in here to talk about Linda. I came in because... well, it's this apartment situation. I know the people in my building are watching my comings and goings. They keep track of when I leave and when I come back, and they're slipping notes about me to the management office. Everyone keeps telling me, \"Oh, they're just routine notices on the doors, everybody gets them,\" but I know better. It's not just notices. They're keeping tabs on me. Just like how the mailman always seems to drop off the letters right when I'm walking out to get the paper. The paper used to be thicker, you know? The Sunday edition would take you all morning to read. Now it's barely a pamphlet. You can read the whole thing in ten minutes. They don't even have the good comic strips anymore. I used to love reading Peanuts. Good ol' Charlie Brown. He never could kick that football. Lucy always pulled it away at the last second. Just like people, I guess. Always pulling the rug out from under you.\n\nDoctor: Before the apartment building situation started, did you usually have trouble trusting other people in general?\n\nPatient: Trusting people in general? Before all this started? Well, I don't know, I guess I trusted people okay. I mean, you have to trust people a little bit, right? Like when you go to the diner, you trust the cook isn't going to spit in your food. I used to go to this diner on 5th and Main, Mel's Diner. Mel was this big guy, always wore a white apron that was never actually white by the end of the day. He made the best meatloaf sandwich. You can't find a good meatloaf sandwich anymore. Everybody wants fancy paninis with arugula and aioli. What even is aioli? It's just mayonnaise with garlic, if you ask me. But they charge you three extra dollars for it. Mel never charged extra for anything. He had this jukebox in the corner, played real records, not CDs or digital stuff. It cost a quarter to play three songs. I always played Johnny Cash. 'Ring of Fire,' usually. Cash had that deep voice, sounded like gravel. They don't make music like that anymore either. Now it's all synthesizers and computers making the beats. It's too loud, too. You go into a store now and you can't even hear yourself think over the music. But yeah, I guess I trusted people fine before. I just went about my business, you know? Never had much trouble with folks until this whole thing with the building started.\n\nDoctor: Before the apartment building situation started, did you usually avoid social or work activities because you feared criticism or rejection from other people?\n\nPatient: Avoid things because of criticism? No, no, I wouldn't say that. I never really worried too much about what people thought of me, you know? I mean, I kept to myself mostly, sure. Never was one for big crowds or parties. Parties are always so loud anyway. People shouting over the music, trying to be heard. I remember going to a party once, must have been back in '85 or '86. It was my cousin Jimmy's housewarming. He bought this little split-level out in the suburbs. Had a built-in barbecue in the backyard, brick, really nice. He made these burgers, said they were his secret recipe. Turned out he just mixed onion soup mix into the ground beef. Everybody thought he was some kind of culinary genius. Onion soup mix! You can get a box of that for, what, a dollar? Used to be fifty cents. Everything's so expensive now. You go to the grocery store for a loaf of bread and a gallon of milk, and suddenly you're out twenty bucks. And the milk goes bad so fast now. I swear, they put something in it to make it spoil quicker so you have to buy more. But no, I never stayed home just because I thought people would reject me or anything like that. I just preferred my own company.\n\nDoctor: Outside of the apartment building issue, do you often find yourself suspicious of different people in many parts of your life?\n\nPatient: Suspicious of different people? No, not really. I mean, I don't go around thinking everybody's out to get me, if that's what you mean. It's just the folks in my building. Outside of that, I don't pay much mind to strangers. Like when I take the bus. I take the 42 down to the pharmacy sometimes. The buses used to run right on time, you could set your watch by them. Now you're waiting twenty, thirty minutes in the freezing cold. And the seats, they have that weird fuzzy fabric with the crazy patterns on them. I read somewhere they design it like that so you can't see how dirty it is. Makes you wonder when they actually wash them. Probably never. My mother used to wash our slipcovers every spring. Had this big wringer washer in the basement. You had to be careful or you'd get your fingers caught in the rollers. Hurt like the dickens. She'd hang them on the line out back, and they'd smell like fresh air and sunshine. You can't get that smell from a bottle of detergent, no matter what the commercials say. They put all those chemicals in the soap now, gives people rashes. But anyway, no, I'm not suspicious of the bus driver or the lady at the pharmacy or anything. It's just my building.\n\nDoctor: Do you often think other people are giving you hidden messages or talking about you in everyday situations outside your apartment building?\n\nPatient: Hidden messages? No, I don't think so. I mean, people talk, sure, but I don't think they're talking about me or trying to send me secret codes or anything. Well, except for the building, like I said. But everywhere else? No. I just watch the news sometimes, the local channel. They always have the same anchors, the guy with the hair that doesn't move and the lady who smiles too much even when she's talking about a house fire. It's unsettling, really. How can you smile about a fire? I knew a guy who lost his house in a fire back in the nineties. Electrical fault, they said. It was one of those old Victorian houses, beautiful woodwork, all gone in a couple of hours. He had this collection of antique clocks, grandfather clocks, cuckoo clocks, you name it. The heat just melted them all together into one big lump of brass and wood. It's a shame. You can't replace history like that. Now they build houses out of cheap pine and drywall, they go up like matchsticks. But no, the news anchors aren't sending me messages. They just read off the teleprompter. You can see their eyes moving back and forth if you look close enough.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

not_in_workforce / 9th_12th_no_diploma\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S019\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Before the apartment building situation started, did you usually avoid social or work activities because you feared criticism or rejection from other people?\n\nPatient: Avoid things because of criticism? No, no, I wouldn't say that. I never really worried too much about what people thought of me, you know? I mean, I kept to myself mostly, sure. Never was one for big crowds or parties. Parties are always so loud anyway. People shouting over the music, trying to be heard. I remember going to a party once, must have been back in '85 or '86. It was my cousin Jimmy's housewarming. He bought this little split-level out in the suburbs. Had a built-in barbecue in the backyard, brick, really nice. He made these burgers, said they were his secret recipe. Turned out he just mixed onion soup mix into the ground beef. Everybody thought he was some kind of culinary genius. Onion soup mix! You can get a box of that for, what, a dollar? Used to be fifty cents. Everything's so expensive now. You go to the grocery store for a loaf of bread and a gallon of milk, and suddenly you're out twenty bucks. And the milk goes bad so fast now. I swear, they put something in it to make it spoil quicker so you have to buy more. But no, I never stayed home just because I thought people would reject me or anything like that. I just preferred my own company.\n\nDoctor: Outside of the apartment building issue, do you often find yourself suspicious of different people in many parts of your life?\n\nPatient: Suspicious of different people? No, not really. I mean, I don't go around thinking everybody's out to get me, if that's what you mean. It's just the folks in my building. Outside of that, I don't pay much mind to strangers. Like when I take the bus. I take the 42 down to the pharmacy sometimes. The buses used to run right on time, you could set your watch by them. Now you're waiting twenty, thirty minutes in the freezing cold. And the seats, they have that weird fuzzy fabric with the crazy patterns on them. I read somewhere they design it like that so you can't see how dirty it is. Makes you wonder when they actually wash them. Probably never. My mother used to wash our slipcovers every spring. Had this big wringer washer in the basement. You had to be careful or you'd get your fingers caught in the rollers. Hurt like the dickens. She'd hang them on the line out back, and they'd smell like fresh air and sunshine. You can't get that smell from a bottle of detergent, no matter what the commercials say. They put all those chemicals in the soap now, gives people rashes. But anyway, no, I'm not suspicious of the bus driver or the lady at the pharmacy or anything. It's just my building.\n\nDoctor: Do you often think other people are giving you hidden messages or talking about you in everyday situations outside your apartment building?\n\nPatient: Hidden messages? No, I don't think so. I mean, people talk, sure, but I don't think they're talking about me or trying to send me secret codes or anything. Well, except for the building, like I said. But everywhere else? No. I just watch the news sometimes, the local channel. They always have the same anchors, the guy with the hair that doesn't move and the lady who smiles too much even when she's talking about a house fire. It's unsettling, really. How can you smile about a fire? I knew a guy who lost his house in a fire back in the nineties. Electrical fault, they said. It was one of those old Victorian houses, beautiful woodwork, all gone in a couple of hours. He had this collection of antique clocks, grandfather clocks, cuckoo clocks, you name it. The heat just melted them all together into one big lump of brass and wood. It's a shame. You can't replace history like that. Now they build houses out of cheap pine and drywall, they go up like matchsticks. But no, the news anchors aren't sending me messages. They just read off the teleprompter. You can see their eyes moving back and forth if you look close enough.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you been this way since early adulthood, with a long-standing pattern of odd or eccentric behavior?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. Odd? Me? No, I wouldn't say that. I've always just been a regular guy, really. Even back when I was young. I left school in the eleventh grade, you know, and went straight into the workforce. Got a job at the old stamping plant on Miller Road. They tore it down a few years ago, put up one of those big self-storage places. Everybody's got too much junk these days, they have to rent a whole separate building just to keep it all. What do you even put in there? Old mattresses and exercise bikes you never use. 

Anyway, at the plant, we used to make these heavy steel brackets for car bumpers. Real steel. The kind that would actually protect you if you got into a fender bender. Now cars are all plastic and fiberglass. You tap a shopping cart in the grocery store parking lot and the whole front end of your car crumples like a soda can. I saw a lady do that just the other week. She was driving one of those little silver hatchbacks, trying to squeeze into a spot, and crunch. Bumper cracked right in half. And the shopping carts are plastic now, too! With the one wheel that always sticks. You're pushing it down the cereal aisle and it keeps pulling to the left, squeaking the whole way. Drives you absolutely crazy when you're just trying to get a box of cornflakes. 

But no, I wasn't eccentric or odd or anything like that. I just worked my shifts and minded my own business.

Well, I mean, I guess I've always been a little careful, you know? You have to be. But not like this whole thing with the apartment building. That's different. But, you know, being on guard... it reminds me of this security guard we had at the hardware store back in '88. Guy's name was Frank. Frank used to walk the aisles like he was patrolling a military base. He had this big, heavy flashlight, the kind that takes like six D batteries. You don't see those much anymore, everything's LED now and rechargeable. Which is fine, I guess, but there was something about the weight of those old flashlights. You felt like you actually had something in your hand. Frank would shine that thing into every dark corner of the stockroom. He was always convinced kids were sneaking in to steal spray paint. Anyway, I wouldn't say I've been suspicious my whole life, no. Just... you know, paying attention to what's going on around me. Like Frank.\n\nDoctor: Have you generally preferred being alone over being with other people?\n\nPatient: Being alone? Yeah, mostly. I mean, I don't really go out of my way to be around crowds or anything. It's just easier that way, you know? Less hassle. I remember back when I was a kid, my old man would drag me to these massive family reunions out by the lake. Hundreds of people, cousins I didn't even know existed. Everyone was always yelling over each other, trying to get to the potato salad. My aunt Martha made the best potato salad, though. She put a little bit of mustard in it, gave it this real nice tang. You can't buy potato salad like that at the deli counter anymore. They always put too much mayonnaise in it now, makes it all soggy and heavy. I tried making it myself a few years back, bought the potatoes, the celery, the whole nine yards. But I ended up boiling the potatoes too long and it just turned into this big bowl of mush. So I just threw it out. But yeah, I guess I do like being by myself most of the time. Just me and my own space.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had unusual beliefs or experiences that other people would say are odd or hard to understand, even outside of this apartment situation?\n\nPatient: Unusual beliefs? No, I don't think so. I mean, I see things the way they are. People might think it's odd that I don't trust the water company, but that's just common sense. Have you seen what they put in the water these days? Fluoride, they say. Back in the day, you just drank from the tap and didn't think twice about it. I remember drinking straight from the garden hose in the summer when I was a kid. The water always tasted a little bit like rubber and dirt, but it was cold. We had this big green hose, must have been fifty feet long. My dad bought it at Sears. Sears used to be the place to go for everything. Tools, appliances, clothes. You could spend a whole Saturday just walking around Sears. I bought a socket set there in '82, Craftsman. Still have it. You can't beat that lifetime warranty. I tried to take a broken ratchet back to one of those new hardware stores last year, and the kid behind the counter looked at me like I had two heads. Didn't even know what a warranty was. Just told me I had to buy a new one. It's all about making a quick buck now. Nobody stands behind their products anymore. So, no, no unusual beliefs. Just... knowing how things used to be better, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Before this last year, did you tend to distrust or suspect other people’s motives for most of your adult life?\n\nPatient: Distrust people? You just did your shift and went home. The line boss there, this guy named Sal, he was always riding everybody. He had this little clipboard he carried around everywhere. Sal thought he was some big shot because he got to wear a white hard hat instead of a yellow one. I never understood why the color of a piece of plastic made someone think they were better than you. Hard hats are just to protect your skull, right? But Sal, he'd strut around... he drove this beat-up old Chevy Nova, faded blue, missing a hubcap on the back right tire. I always noticed that hubcap because I used to park my Ford Fairmont two spots down from him. That Fairmont was a good car. Had the bench seats in the front, the kind you don't see anymore. Everything's bucket seats now with that big center console taking up all the room. You can't even slide over to get out the passenger side if someone parks too close to you. It's ridiculous. No, no, I wouldn't say that. Outside of the building, I mean, people are just people. They're mostly just trying to get through the day, same as anyone else. She had this one pair that looked like little pearls, but they were just plastic. She wore them every Sunday to church. We went to this Methodist church on 4th Street, had these huge stained glass windows. The sun would come through them in the morning and turn the pews all blue and red. The pews were made of this really solid oak, polished so smooth you could slide right off if you weren't paying attention. They don't build churches like that anymore. But everywhere else? No. I just watch the news sometimes, the local channel. They always have the same anchors, the guy with the hair that doesn't move and the lady who smiles too much even when she's talking about a house fire. It's unsettling, really. How can you smile about a fire? I knew a guy who lost his house in a fire back in the nineties. Electrical fault, they said. It was one of those old Victorian houses, beautiful woodwork, all gone in a couple of hours. He had this collection of antique clocks, grandfather clocks, cuckoo clocks, you name it. The heat just melted them all together into one big lump of brass and wood. It's a shame. You can't replace history like that. Now they build houses out of cheap pine and drywall, they go up like matchsticks. But no, the news anchors aren't sending me messages. They just read off the teleprompter. You can see their eyes moving back and forth if you look close enough.\n\nDoctor: Have you been this way since early adulthood, with a long-standing pattern of odd or eccentric behavior?\n\nPatient: Odd? Me? No, I wouldn't say that. I've always just been a regular guy, really. Even back when I was young. I left school in the eleventh grade, you know, and went straight into the workforce. Got a job at the old stamping plant on Miller Road. They tore it down a few years ago, put up one of those big self-storage places. Everybody's got too much junk these days, they have to rent a whole separate building just to keep it all. What do you even put in there? Old mattresses and exercise bikes you never use. \n\nAnyway, at the plant, we used to make these heavy steel brackets for car bumpers. Real steel. The kind that would actually protect you if you got into a fender bender. Now cars are all plastic and fiberglass. You tap a shopping cart in the grocery store parking lot and the whole front end of your car crumples like a soda can. I saw a lady do that just the other week. She was driving one of those little silver hatchbacks, trying to squeeze into a spot, and crunch. Bumper cracked right in half. And the shopping carts are plastic now, too! With the one wheel that always sticks. You're pushing it down the cereal aisle and it keeps pulling to the left, squeaking the whole way. Drives you absolutely crazy when you're just trying to get a box of cornflakes. \n\nBut no, I wasn't eccentric or odd or anything like that. I just worked my shifts and minded my own business.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Out to get me? No, no, I never thought that. Before all this nonsense with the building started, I didn't have any reason to worry about people. Folks just went about their business. Hell, when I was younger, we used to leave the front door wide open in the summer just to get a cross breeze. You didn't even lock the screen door. 

The screen doors back then had those big heavy springs on them, you know? They'd slam shut behind you with a real loud whack. My dad used to yell at me for letting it slam. He worked nights at the foundry, so he slept during the day. Had to have absolute quiet. He put aluminum foil over the bedroom windows to keep the sun out. Worked pretty well, actually, kept the room cool too. Foil is great for that. You see people putting it on their windshields now in the summer, those shiny accordion things. I bought one at the auto parts store a few years back, but it never folded up right after the first time I used it. It's supposed to fold like an accordion, but the cardboard gets bent and then it just flops around. I always ended up just tossing it in the backseat, taking up all that room. 

But yeah, no, I never had any worries about people being after me in general. Never crossed my mind before I moved in here.

I usually hate those places. The aisles are too wide and you can never find anybody to help you. Back when I worked hardware, we actually talked to the customers, you know? We knew what we were talking about. Now it's just teenagers looking at their phones and self-checkout machines barking at you to bag your items. And the tools... don't get me started. I bought this plastic rake because it was all they had, and it was a complete piece of junk. Snapped on me in two weeks. You need solid wood and steel for a real tool. \n\nBut anyway, yeah, it was right around that time, a year and a month ago, that I first caught on to them watching me and slipping those notes around.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever heard voices that other people could not hear?\n\nPatient: Voices? No, I've never heard any voices. Well, except for the neighbors talking through the walls, but that's just because the walls are paper thin in that place. You can hear everything. I remember when I lived in this old brick building downtown back in the 90s, you couldn't hear a peep from anyone. Solid construction, you know? They don't build them like that anymore. Now it's all drywall and cheap insulation. I tried telling the landlord about it, but he just brushed me off. Said it was normal apartment living. Normal? Hah. I know what normal is, and having your neighbors sound like they're in your living room isn't it. I used to do some framing work myself, before the hardware store. We always made sure to use the right materials. But no, no voices that aren't there, just the real ones that are way too loud.\n\nDoctor: Have you generally been suspicious of other people or on guard about their motives for most of your adult life?\n\nPatient: Suspicious? Well, I mean, I guess I've always been a little careful, you know? You have to be. But not like this whole thing with the apartment building. That's different. But, you know, being on guard... it reminds me of this security guard we had at the hardware store back in '88. Guy's name was Frank. Frank used to walk the aisles like he was patrolling a military base. He had this big, heavy flashlight, the kind that takes like six D batteries. You don't see those much anymore, everything's LED now and rechargeable. Which is fine, I guess, but there was something about the weight of those old flashlights. You felt like you actually had something in your hand. Frank would shine that thing into every dark corner of the stockroom. He was always convinced kids were sneaking in to steal spray paint. Anyway, I wouldn't say I've been suspicious my whole life, no. Just... you know, paying attention to what's going on around me. Like Frank.\n\nDoctor: Have you generally preferred being alone over being with other people?\n\nPatient: Being alone? Yeah, mostly. I mean, I don't really go out of my way to be around crowds or anything. It's just easier that way, you know? Less hassle. I remember back when I was a kid, my old man would drag me to these massive family reunions out by the lake. Hundreds of people, cousins I didn't even know existed. Everyone was always yelling over each other, trying to get to the potato salad. My aunt Martha made the best potato salad, though. She put a little bit of mustard in it, gave it this real nice tang. You can't buy potato salad like that at the deli counter anymore. They always put too much mayonnaise in it now, makes it all soggy and heavy. I tried making it myself a few years back, bought the potatoes, the celery, the whole nine yards. But I ended up boiling the potatoes too long and it just turned into this big bowl of mush. So I just threw it out. But yeah, I guess I do like being by myself most of the time. Just me and my own space.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had unusual beliefs or experiences that other people would say are odd or hard to understand, even outside of this apartment situation?\n\nPatient: Unusual beliefs? No, I don't think so. I mean, I see things the way they are. People might think it's odd that I don't trust the water company, but that's just common sense. Have you seen what they put in the water these days? Fluoride, they say. Back in the day, you just drank from the tap and didn't think twice about it. I remember drinking straight from the garden hose in the summer when I was a kid. The water always tasted a little bit like rubber and dirt, but it was cold. We had this big green hose, must have been fifty feet long. My dad bought it at Sears. Sears used to be the place to go for everything. Tools, appliances, clothes. You could spend a whole Saturday just walking around Sears. I bought a socket set there in '82, Craftsman. Still have it. You can't beat that lifetime warranty. I tried to take a broken ratchet back to one of those new hardware stores last year, and the kid behind the counter looked at me like I had two heads. Didn't even know what a warranty was. Just told me I had to buy a new one. It's all about making a quick buck now. Nobody stands behind their products anymore. So, no, no unusual beliefs. Just... knowing how things used to be better, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Before this last year, did you tend to distrust or suspect other people’s motives for most of your adult life?\n\nPatient: Distrust people? Most of my life? No, I wouldn't say that. I mean, I've always just minded my own business, you know? Kept my head down. It's like when I used to work at the bottling plant back in the early nineties. You just did your shift and went home. The line boss there, this guy named Sal, he was always riding everybody. He had this little clipboard he carried around everywhere. Sal thought he was some big shot because he got to wear a white hard hat instead of a yellow one. I never understood why the color of a piece of plastic made someone think they were better than you. Hard hats are just to protect your skull, right? But Sal, he'd strut around... he drove this beat-up old Chevy Nova, faded blue, missing a hubcap on the back right tire. I always noticed that hubcap because I used to park my Ford Fairmont two spots down from him. That Fairmont was a good car. Had the bench seats in the front, the kind you don't see anymore. Everything's bucket seats now with that big center console taking up all the room. You can't even slide over to get out the passenger side if someone parks too close to you. It's ridiculous. But no, I didn't really distrust people before all this started. Just... you know, I just kept to myself mostly.\n\nDoctor: Outside of the apartment building situation, do you often believe that other people are trying to deceive, harm, or take advantage of you?\n\nPatient: Deceive or harm me? No, no, I wouldn't say that. Outside of the building, I mean, people are just people. They're mostly just trying to get through the day, same as anyone else. Like the checkout girl at the grocery store down the street, she's always ringing things up so fast. She wears these big hoop earrings, the silver kind. I always wonder if those get heavy pulling on your ears all day. My mother used to wear clip-on earrings. She had this whole jewelry box full of them, velvet-lined, smelled like old perfume and dust. She had this one pair that looked like little pearls, but they were just plastic. She wore them every Sunday to church. We went to this Methodist church on 4th Street, had these huge stained glass windows. The sun would come through them in the morning and turn the pews all blue and red. The pews were made of this really solid oak, polished so smooth you could slide right off if you weren't paying attention. They don't build churches like that anymore. Now they look like office buildings, all drywall and fluorescent lights. It just doesn't feel the same, you know? But no, I don't think people are trying to take advantage of me or anything. Not usually.\n\nDoctor: Before the apartment building situation started, did you ever have a strong, fixed belief that someone close to you was being unfaithful or deceiving you?\n\nPatient: Unfaithful? No, no, I've never been married, so there was nobody to be unfaithful. I had a girlfriend once, a long time ago, Linda. She worked at the bakery downtown, the one that used to sell those big bear claws with the almond paste inside. I haven't had a good bear claw in years. The bakeries now, they just sell those dry scones that taste like cardboard. Anyway, Linda was a good girl, never gave me a reason to think she was doing anything wrong. \n\nBut I didn't come in here to talk about Linda. I came in because... well, it's this apartment situation. I know the people in my building are watching my comings and goings. They keep track of when I leave and when I come back, and they're slipping notes about me to the management office. Everyone keeps telling me, \"Oh, they're just routine notices on the doors, everybody gets them,\" but I know better. It's not just notices. They're keeping tabs on me. Just like how the mailman always seems to drop off the letters right when I'm walking out to get the paper. The paper used to be thicker, you know? The Sunday edition would take you all morning to read. Now it's barely a pamphlet. You can read the whole thing in ten minutes. They don't even have the good comic strips anymore. I used to love reading Peanuts. Good ol' Charlie Brown. He never could kick that football. Lucy always pulled it away at the last second. Just like people, I guess. Always pulling the rug out from under you.\n\nDoctor: Before the apartment building situation started, did you usually have trouble trusting other people in general?\n\nPatient: Trusting people in general? Before all this started? Well, I don't know, I guess I trusted people okay. I mean, you have to trust people a little bit, right? Like when you go to the diner, you trust the cook isn't going to spit in your food. I used to go to this diner on 5th and Main, Mel's Diner. Mel was this big guy, always wore a white apron that was never actually white by the end of the day. He made the best meatloaf sandwich. You can't find a good meatloaf sandwich anymore. Everybody wants fancy paninis with arugula and aioli. What even is aioli? It's just mayonnaise with garlic, if you ask me. But they charge you three extra dollars for it. Mel never charged extra for anything. He had this jukebox in the corner, played real records, not CDs or digital stuff. It cost a quarter to play three songs. I always played Johnny Cash. 'Ring of Fire,' usually. Cash had that deep voice, sounded like gravel. They don't make music like that anymore either. Now it's all synthesizers and computers making the beats. It's too loud, too. You go into a store now and you can't even hear yourself think over the music. But yeah, I guess I trusted people fine before. I just went about my business, you know? Never had much trouble with folks until this whole thing with the building started.\n\nDoctor: Before the apartment building situation started, did you usually avoid social or work activities because you feared criticism or rejection from other people?\n\nPatient: Avoid things because of criticism? No, no, I wouldn't say that. I never really worried too much about what people thought of me, you know? I mean, I kept to myself mostly, sure. Never was one for big crowds or parties. Parties are always so loud anyway. People shouting over the music, trying to be heard. I remember going to a party once, must have been back in '85 or '86. It was my cousin Jimmy's housewarming. He bought this little split-level out in the suburbs. Had a built-in barbecue in the backyard, brick, really nice. He made these burgers, said they were his secret recipe. Turned out he just mixed onion soup mix into the ground beef. Everybody thought he was some kind of culinary genius. Onion soup mix! You can get a box of that for, what, a dollar? Used to be fifty cents. Everything's so expensive now. You go to the grocery store for a loaf of bread and a gallon of milk, and suddenly you're out twenty bucks. And the milk goes bad so fast now. I swear, they put something in it to make it spoil quicker so you have to buy more. But no, I never stayed home just because I thought people would reject me or anything like that. I just preferred my own company.\n\nDoctor: Outside of the apartment building issue, do you often find yourself suspicious of different people in many parts of your life?\n\nPatient: Suspicious of different people? No, not really. I mean, I don't go around thinking everybody's out to get me, if that's what you mean. It's just the folks in my building. Outside of that, I don't pay much mind to strangers. Like when I take the bus. I take the 42 down to the pharmacy sometimes. The buses used to run right on time, you could set your watch by them. Now you're waiting twenty, thirty minutes in the freezing cold. And the seats, they have that weird fuzzy fabric with the crazy patterns on them. I read somewhere they design it like that so you can't see how dirty it is. Makes you wonder when they actually wash them. Probably never. My mother used to wash our slipcovers every spring. Had this big wringer washer in the basement. You had to be careful or you'd get your fingers caught in the rollers. Hurt like the dickens. She'd hang them on the line out back, and they'd smell like fresh air and sunshine. You can't get that smell from a bottle of detergent, no matter what the commercials say. They put all those chemicals in the soap now, gives people rashes. But anyway, no, I'm not suspicious of the bus driver or the lady at the pharmacy or anything. It's just my building.\n\nDoctor: Do you often think other people are giving you hidden messages or talking about you in everyday situations outside your apartment building?\n\nPatient: Hidden messages? No, I don't think so. I mean, people talk, sure, but I don't think they're talking about me or trying to send me secret codes or anything. Well, except for the building, like I said. But everywhere else? No. I just watch the news sometimes, the local channel. They always have the same anchors, the guy with the hair that doesn't move and the lady who smiles too much even when she's talking about a house fire. It's unsettling, really. How can you smile about a fire? I knew a guy who lost his house in a fire back in the nineties. Electrical fault, they said. It was one of those old Victorian houses, beautiful woodwork, all gone in a couple of hours. He had this collection of antique clocks, grandfather clocks, cuckoo clocks, you name it. The heat just melted them all together into one big lump of brass and wood. It's a shame. You can't replace history like that. Now they build houses out of cheap pine and drywall, they go up like matchsticks. But no, the news anchors aren't sending me messages. They just read off the teleprompter. You can see their eyes moving back and forth if you look close enough.\n\nDoctor: Have you been this way since early adulthood, with a long-standing pattern of odd or eccentric behavior?\n\nPatient: Odd? Me? No, I wouldn't say that. I've always just been a regular guy, really. Even back when I was young. I left school in the eleventh grade, you know, and went straight into the workforce. Got a job at the old stamping plant on Miller Road. They tore it down a few years ago, put up one of those big self-storage places. Everybody's got too much junk these days, they have to rent a whole separate building just to keep it all. What do you even put in there? Old mattresses and exercise bikes you never use. \n\nAnyway, at the plant, we used to make these heavy steel brackets for car bumpers. Real steel. The kind that would actually protect you if you got into a fender bender. Now cars are all plastic and fiberglass. You tap a shopping cart in the grocery store parking lot and the whole front end of your car crumples like a soda can. I saw a lady do that just the other week. She was driving one of those little silver hatchbacks, trying to squeeze into a spot, and crunch. Bumper cracked right in half. And the shopping carts are plastic now, too! With the one wheel that always sticks. You're pushing it down the cereal aisle and it keeps pulling to the left, squeaking the whole way. Drives you absolutely crazy when you're just trying to get a box of cornflakes. \n\nBut no, I wasn't eccentric or odd or anything like that. I just worked my shifts and minded my own business.\n\nDoctor: Before the apartment building situation started, did you ever worry that people in general might be out to get you?\n\nPatient: Out to get me? No, no, I never thought that. Before all this nonsense with the building started, I didn't have any reason to worry about people. Folks just went about their business. Hell, when I was younger, we used to leave the front door wide open in the summer just to get a cross breeze. You didn't even lock the screen door. \n\nThe screen doors back then had those big heavy springs on them, you know? They'd slam shut behind you with a real loud whack. My dad used to yell at me for letting it slam. He worked nights at the foundry, so he slept during the day. Had to have absolute quiet. He put aluminum foil over the bedroom windows to keep the sun out. Worked pretty well, actually, kept the room cool too. Foil is great for that. You see people putting it on their windshields now in the summer, those shiny accordion things. I bought one at the auto parts store a few years back, but it never folded up right after the first time I used it. It's supposed to fold like an accordion, but the cardboard gets bent and then it just flops around. I always ended up just tossing it in the backseat, taking up all that room. \n\nBut yeah, no, I never had any worries about people being after me in general. Never crossed my mind before I moved in here.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Me? No, I wouldn't say that. I've always just been a regular guy, really. Even back when I was young. I left school in the eleventh grade, you know, and went straight into the workforce. Got a job at the old stamping plant on Miller Road. They tore it down a few years ago, put up one of those big self-storage places. Everybody's got too much junk these days, they have to rent a whole separate building just to keep it all. What do you even put in there? Old mattresses and exercise bikes you never use. \n\nAnyway, at the plant, we used to make these heavy steel brackets for car bumpers. Real steel. The kind that would actually protect you if you got into a fender bender. Now cars are all plastic and fiberglass. You tap a shopping cart in the grocery store parking lot and the whole front end of your car crumples like a soda can. I saw a lady do that just the other week. She was driving one of those little silver hatchbacks, trying to squeeze into a spot, and crunch. Bumper cracked right in half. And the shopping carts are plastic now, too! With the one wheel that always sticks. You're pushing it down the cereal aisle and it keeps pulling to the left, squeaking the whole way. Drives you absolutely crazy when you're just trying to get a box of cornflakes. \n\nBut no, I wasn't eccentric or odd or anything like that. I just worked my shifts and minded my own business.\n\nDoctor: Before the apartment building situation started, did you ever worry that people in general might be out to get you?\n\nPatient: Out to get me? No, no, I never thought that. Before all this nonsense with the building started, I didn't have any reason to worry about people. Folks just went about their business. Hell, when I was younger, we used to leave the front door wide open in the summer just to get a cross breeze. You didn't even lock the screen door. \n\nThe screen doors back then had those big heavy springs on them, you know? They'd slam shut behind you with a real loud whack. My dad used to yell at me for letting it slam. He worked nights at the foundry, so he slept during the day. Had to have absolute quiet. He put aluminum foil over the bedroom windows to keep the sun out. Worked pretty well, actually, kept the room cool too. Foil is great for that. You see people putting it on their windshields now in the summer, those shiny accordion things. I bought one at the auto parts store a few years back, but it never folded up right after the first time I used it. It's supposed to fold like an accordion, but the cardboard gets bent and then it just flops around. I always ended up just tossing it in the backseat, taking up all that room. \n\nBut yeah, no, I never had any worries about people being after me in general. Never crossed my mind before I moved in here.\n\n[Last Question]\nBefore age 18, did you already tend to be suspicious of other people’s motives?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 59 / not_in_workforce / 9th_12th_no_diploma\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Suspicious? When I was a kid? No, I wouldn't say that. I mean, I trusted folks just fine. We all played stickball in the street and traded baseball cards. You didn't have to worry about people trying to pull one over on you back then. I remember I had this Mickey Mantle card, a real beauty, crisp corners and everything. I traded it for a handful of marbles and a broken yo-yo because I just really wanted that yo-yo. It was one of those wooden ones, painted bright red. Duncan, I think the brand was. You don't see wooden yo-yos much anymore, they're all plastic now with lights inside them that flash when they spin. It's too much. The batteries always die after a week anyway, and then it's just a heavy piece of plastic that doesn't even sleep right. You know how to make a yo-yo sleep? You gotta throw it down hard but keep your wrist loose. My older brother taught me that. He was always good at tricks, could do 'Walk the Dog' and 'Around the World' like it was nothing. He joined the Navy right out of high school, went off to see the world for real. Sent me postcards from all over—Japan, Italy, places I couldn't even pronounce. I kept them in a shoebox under my bed. An old Buster Brown shoebox. Remember those? They had the little boy and his dog inside the lid. But no, I wasn't suspicious of anybody. Just a regular kid trying to get his yo-yo to work.
